# Abstract

Between Stalin’s death in March 1953 and the dissolution of the USSR in
December 1991, the urban population of the seven western and northern
union republics - the Russian SFSR and the Ukrainian, Byelorussian,
Lithuanian, Latvian, Estonian and Moldavian SSRs - grew by nearly
three-fifths from 120,98M at the 1959 census to 193,14M in 1989 across
the catalogued settlement network. Alongside the steady growth of the
republican capitals and older provincial centres, post-Stalin economic
planning created hundreds of new towns: Volga, Dnieper and
Angara-Yenisei hydroelectric cities, automotive and petrochemical
monocities, Moscow science cities, closed nuclear and naval towns
(*ZATO*) and Arctic oil, gas, diamond and railway settlements across
West Siberia, Yakutia and the Baikal-Amur Mainline.

This gazetteer traces the population histories of 7.783 towns,
urban-type settlements and large villages across all 41 regions from
1953 to 1991. Together with the four post-war Soviet censuses of 1959,
1970, 1979 and 1989 and the opening (1953) and closing (1991)
benchmarks, the tables record the pre-evacuation populations of towns
inside the 1986 Chernobyl Exclusion Zone and the declassified headcounts
of the secret nuclear and naval cities omitted from contemporary Soviet
publications.

# Methodology

The 7.783 settlements in this gazetteer span all 41 regions of the
Estonian SSR (Region 1), Latvian SSR (Region 2), Lithuanian SSR (Region
3), Moldavian SSR (Region 4), the Kaliningrad Oblast RSFSR exclave
(Region 5), the Byelorussian SSR (Regions 6-8), the Ukrainian SSR
(Regions 9-15) and the mainland RSFSR (Regions 16-41)
[\[1\]](#ref-tssu_1962_perepis_1959)[\[2\]](#ref-tssu_1972_perepis_1970)[\[3\]](#ref-tssu_1984_perepis_1979)[\[4\]](#ref-goskomstat_1991_perepis_1989)[\[5\]](#ref-goskomstat_1992_demograficheskiy_ezhegodnik_1991).
Population figures are drawn from the All-Union census returns of
January 1959, January 1970, January 1979 and January 1989, supplemented
by TsSU and Goskomstat urban registers for 1953 and 1991, post-Soviet
declassified *Minsredmash* series for closed nuclear and naval cities,
and April 1986 pre-evacuation counts for settlements evacuated after the
Chernobyl accident
[\[6\]](#ref-lappo_1997_geografiya_gorodov)[\[7\]](#ref-rowland_1996_russia_secret_cities_zato)[\[8\]](#ref-iaea_2006_chernobyl_environmental_consequences).

The regional tables in the Results section group the towns into four
geographic zones: the 367 settlements of the Baltic SSRs, the Moldavian
SSR and Kaliningrad Oblast (Regions 1-5); the 2.347 settlements of the
Byelorussian and Ukrainian SSRs (Regions 6-15); the 3.900 settlements of
European Russia, the Volga-Ural republics and the North Caucasus
(Regions 16-30); and the 1.169 settlements of Siberia, Yakutia and the
Far East (Regions 31-41). The Appendix then presents a 41-region urban
population summary summing the complete 1959-1989 Soviet census series
alongside 1953 and 1991 benchmarks.

# Results

## Baltic SSRs, Moldavian SSR and Kaliningrad Exclave

In the western borderlands (Regions 1-5), urban growth followed
contrasting paths: heavy industrial migration into Tallinn, Riga, the
Ida-Virumaa oil-shale basin and the left-bank Dniester towns of
Tiraspol, Bender and Ribnita, balanced ten-pole regional urbanisation
across Lithuania, and naval and fishing expansion in Kaliningrad Oblast
(Table ).

Populations of 371 republican capitals, regional centres, industrial
towns and closed naval or nuclear settlements across Estonia, Latvia,
Lithuania, Moldavia and Kaliningrad Oblast from 1953 to 1991.

## Byelorussian and Ukrainian SSRs

Across Belarus and Ukraine (Regions 6-15), post-war urban growth was led
by Minsk, Kyiv, the western oblast capitals, the Dnieper hydroelectric
and metallurgical bend and the Black Sea ports, while the April 1986
Chernobyl accident forced the evacuation of Pripyat, Chernobyl and
Poliske and the construction of Slavutych (Table ).

Populations of 2.602 settlements across the Byelorussian and Ukrainian
SSRs (Regions 6-15) from 1953 to 1991, including 1986 pre-evacuation
figures for towns in the Chernobyl Exclusion Zone.

## European Russia, Volga-Urals and the North Caucasus

In the European RSFSR, the Volga-Ural republics and the North Caucasus
(Regions 16-30), metropolitan expansion in Moscow and Leningrad, the
Volga-Kama automotive and petrochemical boomtowns (Tolyatti, Naberezhnye
Chelny, Nizhnekamsk and Volzhsky), the Ural closed nuclear towns and the
restored capitals of Elista, Grozny, Nalchik and Cherkessk contrasted
with rural contraction in the surrounding forest and black-earth
villages (Table ).

Populations of 4.383 metropolitan centres, oblast and ASSR capitals,
science cities, hydroelectric monocities and closed nuclear towns across
European Russia, the Volga-Urals and the North Caucasus (Regions 16-30)
from 1953 to 1991.

## Siberia, Yakutia and the Far East

East of the Urals (Regions 31-41), older Trans-Siberian and Kuzbass
industrial cities were joined by the oil and gas boomtowns of the Middle
Ob and Yamal (Surgut, Nizhnevartovsk, Nefteyugansk, Novy Urengoy,
Noyabrsk and Nadym), the Angara-Yenisei hydroelectric cities (Bratsk,
Angarsk, Ust-Ilimsk, Divnogorsk and Sayanogorsk), the diamond and coal
towns of Yakutia (Mirny, Udachny and Neryungri) and the hubs of the
Baikal-Amur Mainline and Pacific coast (Table ).

Populations of 1.258 cities and resource-frontier towns across West
Siberia, the Angara-Yenisei basin, Tuva, Buryatia, Yakutia, the
Baikal-Amur Mainline and the Russian Far East (Regions 31-41) from 1953
to 1991.

# Conclusion

Across the 7.783 settlements catalogued in this gazetteer, the
post-Stalin decades marked the peak of Soviet urban and frontier
expansion. Between 1953 and 1991, rural out-migration from the European
forest and forest-steppe zones and state investment in automotive,
hydroelectric, nuclear and hydrocarbon complexes created a dense
hierarchy of regional capitals and specialised industrial towns. With
the end of the USSR in December 1991 and the withdrawal of northern wage
and transport subsidies, many Arctic mining towns and single-industry
*monogoroda* entered demographic contraction, making the 1989-1991
benchmarks the historical high point of the late-Soviet urban network.
